# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-26

## LLM Agents

| **[VETTA: Coordinating Turn- and Token-Level Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08402v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn LLM agents often receive sparse task feedback across several interactions, while generating each response token by token. This creates two related credit-assignment questions: which responses helped achieve the outcome, and which generation decisions mattered within each response? Existing methods typically focus on only one level: turn-level methods evaluate complete responses but do not distinguish the decisions within them; token-level methods can propagate feedback across turns but do not explicitly model credit for each response. These complementary limitations motivate learning credit at both levels and coordinating it in a single policy update. We introduce VETTA, a credit assignment method that jointly learns turn- and token-level values through separate heads on a shared lightweight critic. VETTA computes advantages along both temporal sequences and combines each turn advantage with a within-response-centered token residual for PPO updates. Furthermore, to reduce value-learning cost, the critic retains only early Transformer blocks from the pretrained checkpoint used to initialize the actor. Experiments on four agent benchmarks demonstrate VETTA's strong performance. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, VETTA achieves success rates of 95.5% on ALFWorld, 76.0% on WebShop, and 52.2% on ScienceWorld. With Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct, it reaches 84.9% and 80.7% success on Sokoban under sparse and dense rewards, respectively. Critic-depth comparisons further show strong task performance with substantially lower critic-side computation. These results demonstrate that coordinating turn- and token-level credit with a lightweight shared critic improves agent performance while keeping value estimation efficient. Code is available at https://github.com/Jiaju-Chen/VETTA-official.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 6 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[Do "True" and "False" Add Up to One? Complement Consistency in LLM-Agent Opinion Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32874v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In LLM-agent opinion simulations, each agent reports the probability that a claim is true after seeing its own previous probability and the probabilities reported by its neighbors, and that number becomes its state in the next round. If the number describes the agent's position rather than the sentence it was shown, then rewriting the claim as its complement and replacing every probability p with 1 - p should return the complementary answer, so the two reports should sum to one. We test this with three small open models: Qwen3-4B, Llama-3.2-3B and Gemma-3-4B. On 2,304 agent states taken from a running network simulation, Qwen's paired reports sum to 1.18 on average, and on 48 real fact-checking claims from AVeriTeC they again exceed one. A controlled benchmark of 24 worlds, each with exactly two possible outcomes, shows the same failure in all three models. The discrepancy is two to ten times larger than the change produced by a same-meaning paraphrase, and it persists under repeated sampling and across peer group sizes and report strengths. When the reports are fed back into two populations that start from complementary states, the populations do not remain mirror images: their divergence is positive after five rounds for every model. Averaging each answer with the complement of its paired answer restores consistency by construction and lowers Brier loss averaged over the two wordings, but at the final round it beats the better of the two original wordings for only one of the three models. A schedule-by-targeting experiment on the same model gives a small, stable interaction because such sweeps keep the claim wording fixed. Consistency under rewording therefore needs its own test before simulated probabilities are read as beliefs</p></details> |  |
| **[J-Comm: Jacobian-Guided Latent Communication between LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32871v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Latent communication requires both an effective continuous reasoning process and an interface through which an external model can leverage its intermediate computation. We present J-Comm, a framework comprising two core components. First, a training-free Geometric Agreement for Latent Reasoning (GEAR) reweights token candidates over existing model embeddings, which monotonically optimizes geometric consensus among candidate tokens in embedding space at each step. Second, we introduce an H+J (Hidden-and-Jacobian) communication interface that gives the Receiver two complementary views of the Sender's reasoning. The H channel supplies continuous inputs derived from feedback distributions, while the J channel conveys intermediate-state cues in vocabulary-indexed Jacobian-lens coordinates. Intuitively, H provides inputs for the Receiver to reason from, while J offers additional guidance within its computation. The Receiver reconstructs these cues using its own dictionary and injects them into intermediate layers during prefill, enabling communication without sharing key-value caches or requiring identical model widths. In Qwen3-8B to Qwen3-4B communication, J-Comm achieves the highest mean score across seven benchmarks, including 94.80% on MATH500, 54.55% on GPQA-Diamond, 73.33% on AIME2024+2025, and 62.93% success on ALFWorld. Compared with TextMAS, it improves performance by 1.29-8.59 percentage points and reduces generated text tokens by 19.2-74.8%.</p></details> |  |
| **[ActionRAG: Evidence-Refining Graph Diffusion for Long-Term Memory in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32752v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents need long-term memory that can recover precise and mutually consistent evidence from growing interaction histories, particularly when answers depend on relations among facts rather than isolated semantic matches. Current memory pipelines remain vulnerable at two stages: indexing errors can fragment repeated entities or merge distinct ones, and online retrieval often treats initial similarity scores as final even when the surrounding graph provides evidence for revising them. We introduce ActionRAG, a graph memory architecture that couples cautious indexing with query-time graph retroevaluation. Its indexing pipeline reconciles type labels and applies tiered entity resolution. Given a query, dual-channel entity and triple retrieval forms an initial evidence set and induces a bounded local graph. Query-conditioned receiver-normalized diffusion refines triple scores using local path structure. Seed protection, hub-penalized flux, and triple materialization preserve direct evidence while recovering missed connections. We evaluate ActionRAG on MemoryAgentBench (MAB), which tests accurate retrieval, conflict resolution, long-range understanding, and test-time learning. Across its eight deterministic-scoring columns, ActionRAG achieves 55.1 average accuracy, improving over the strongest retrieval-based memory baseline by 5.4 points. Under MAB's query-time cost accounting, mean synthesis cost stays below \$0.001 per query on the four reported cost columns, and the sum of separately measured retrieval-side p99 components is 440.7 ms on our consumer Apple Silicon setup.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Butterfly Defects: Tracing Cooperation in LLM Agent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32726v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) increasingly operate as interconnected agents, a fundamental question emerges: does individual cooperation translate into collective cooperation? We investigate this question through the Iterated Prisoner's Dilemma (IPD), moving from dyadic interactions to homogeneous and heterogeneous networks of open-weight LLMs. Our findings reveal that cooperation is highly sensitive to model composition and seemingly minor linguistic changes. Models that cooperate with identical peers can shift toward defection in mixed populations. A simple uncertainty cue can increase or decrease cooperation, redistribute costs, and affect unexposed agents several hops away. Even reversing action-label order can dramatically alter behavior, with uncertainty framing sometimes amplifying this sensitivity. Together, these findings challenge the notion of cooperation as a stable model-level property, revealing how strategic behavior depends on interaction partners, network positions, and the language used to frame decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Probe Before You Edit: Probing-Guided Molecular Optimization for LLM Agents in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00555v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design increasingly employs LLM agents to iteratively refine ligands against a target pocket, yet a viable ligand must satisfy two often-conflicting objectives---binding affinity and druggability---which single optimization steps rarely improve together. To quantify this difficulty, we introduce two diagnostic metrics: the first measures how often a single edit improves both objectives, and the second measures how often a gain on one objective comes with a loss on the other. Applying these diagnostics to current LLM-agent pipelines exposes a consistent failure mode: the agent performs molecular editing without knowing how the pocket-ligand complex responds to local modifications, thus rarely achieving joint improvement. Inspired by medicinal chemists, who probe the pocket-ligand complex with controlled analog edits before choosing an optimization direction, we propose \textbf{PROBE}, an optimization framework built around edit--response probing. PROBE first decomposes the ligand into editable sites and builds a pocket-specific \textbf{site map} that flags where joint gains are plausible, where the two objectives are likely in tension, and where liability substructures should be changed; it then performs controlled probe edits whose responses are distilled into an \textbf{EditManual}. Guided by the site map and EditManual, PROBE runs an iterative multi-agent optimization loop. On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark, PROBE achieves state-of-the-art performance and substantially mitigates the failure modes exposed by our diagnostics metrics. Codes are in https://github.com/yzf-code/PROBE/tree/main</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Revised manuscript</p></details> |
| **[What Transfers Across Protein Pockets? Experience Reuse in Molecular Optimization for Agentic Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32620v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents optimize a ligand in a protein pocket by editing it step by step and docking each edit. Recent agents first spend extra dockings probing the complex with controlled edits; guidance induced from these probes is reported to help more than the chemical knowledge the model already carries. The probes are then discarded with the instance that produced them, so the obvious next step is to keep them in an experience base, letting a new pocket inherit what earlier ones already paid for. Such a base is read through a \emph{key}: the record properties under which past edits are pooled and a new one is looked up. Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. That is not because the target is irrelevant: naming it outright separates them several times more sharply. For a pocket absent from the base, transfer must rely on a description rather than its identity. Among the exact-match descriptions we evaluate, the more specific ones rarely recur, whereas the coarser ones provide little separation; the signals that do transfer primarily describe the edit and the molecule it produced rather than the target. Under the representations and lookup scheme studied here, an experience base may be more useful for prioritizing an agent's own proposals for docking than for directly prescribing what to build.</p></details> |  |
| **[Tool-Calling LLM Agents Under Realistic MCP Faults: Scale Does Not Purchase Fault Tolerance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32555v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly rely on external tools exposed via the Model Context Protocol (MCP), yet current benchmarks assume tools respond correctly and on time, leaving agent resilience to realistic tool failures untested. We introduce MCP-Injector, a drop-in, protocol-aware fault-injection middleware that interposes on the MCP tool-call path with controlled persistence (permanent, transient, intermittent) and seedable schedules. Its fault model is calibrated to the observed incident distribution (failure mode x persistence) of the public MCP ecosystem, mined from 57,240 issue and pull-request threads across 1,774 open-source MCP repositories. In a 1,350-cell experiment crossing two backbone sizes (20B, 120B), three agent stacks (custom multi-round plan-and-execute, OpenAI Agents SDK, LangChain), and three fault injection conditions, we find that faults drawn from this permanent-heavy stress profile cause severe quality degradation (task-completion score falls by 60%, from 8.08 to 3.23 out of 10), and that scale does not purchase fault tolerance for the two gpt-oss backbones we test: the 120B retains no larger share of its fault-free score under faults (resilience-ratio model, $p = 0.96$), and its completion rate under faults is, if anything, lower (91.1% vs. 95.6%; difference 4.4 points, 95% CI $[-0.2, 9.3]$). Agent stacks differ in how well they absorb faults, which suggests that framework-level error handling matters more than model scale. These results demonstrate that capability benchmarks alone can mask operational brittleness, motivating protocol-aware fault injection as a complementary evaluation dimension for tool-calling agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster). 10 pages of main text plus appendices and the NeurIPS checklist. Code and data: https://gitlab.com/uniluxembourg/snt/sedan/papers/mcp-injector (archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23573765)</p></details> |
| **[When Agents Fail: Model or Harness--Who's to Blame?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32483v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents combine models with execution harnesses, so agentic benchmark performance reflects their joint behavior. We argue that harnesses should therefore be evaluated through their interactions with underlying models. We develop a framework combining five complementary measures: (i) Valid Completion Rate (VCR), how reliably agents finish normally and deliver evaluable results, irrespective of task correctness; (ii) Model-Harness Fit, how well a harness supports task achievement through valid completion for a given model; (iii) Cross-Model Fit, a harness's overall suitability across evaluated models; (iv) Interaction Residuals, pairing-specific execution strengths and weaknesses hidden by component-level summaries; and (v) Token Load, the token demands of agent execution, including failed attempts. We evaluate four general-purpose harnesses (OpenCode, Codex CLI, Pi CLI, and DeepAgents) with four core models, plus two fixed native model-harness systems, on six benchmarks covering context management, tool use, long-context planning, multimodal interaction, and artifact handoff. Including native references and stress conditions, 111 evaluation settings yield 52,168 trajectories. Harness rankings vary across models and benchmarks; task performance and valid completion can favor different pairings, and similar task scores can accompany distinct execution profiles. OpenCode ranks first in cross-model performance, while DeepSeek V4 Flash Preview maintains consistently high Mean VCR across harnesses on the shared four-benchmark panel. These findings support evaluating model-harness interactions, training transferable harness-use capabilities in models, and harness designs adapted to the model and task.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint under review</p></details> |
| **[CauProphet: Teaching Agents to Develop Causal Cognition like Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32477v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly support long-horizon tasks by leveraging external memory and past experience. However, existing agents mainly retrieve records or summaries, lacking the ability to form and refine causal understanding of the world. Inspired by human causal cognition, we introduce CauProphet, an agent that develops and updates causal knowledge within its Causal Cognition. For each task, CauProphet combines relevant knowledge in Causal Cognition with current evidence to construct a Causal Belief, a structured belief capturing causal factors and the effects of possible actions. It uses this belief for decision-making and continuously updates both the belief and Causal Cognition as new observations emerge, enabling causal knowledge that persists beyond individual experiences. Evaluations demonstrate that CauProphet progressively acquires causal knowledge and transfers it to future decision-making. In financial research, CauProphet leverages prior discoveries to improve downstream analyses and outperforms all baselines in mean rank correlation with next-day returns, achieving a 25.4% improvement over a baseline agent. It also improves prediction accuracy from 21.3% to 28.6% in dynamic real-world settings. Further experiments validate its ability to perform reliable causal analysis and action selection across scientific and everyday tasks, demonstrating the potential of causal experience learning for next-generation LLM agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[An LLM agent for end-to-end computational materials discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20434v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coordinating multi-scale tasks is an effective strategy for computational materials discovery, yet the diverse algorithms and tools involved make this process highly challenging. Here we present DREAMS, a large language model (LLM)-based agent system capable of executing the entire screening pipeline for metal-organic frameworks (MOFs). DREAMS processes a large body of MOF literature, links publications to their crystal structures, and curates them into a computation-ready database, which is then screened with progressively more expensive simulations. The promising candidates identified for separation under wet flue gas conditions all originate from independent studies. By bridging the heterogeneous stages from literature mining to high-throughput simulation, DREAMS operates across application domains and enables even non-experts to discover high-performance materials in an end-to-end, automated manner.</p></details> |  |
| **[SCI-AGENT-EXAM: Benchmarking Agents on Scientific Research Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32441v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly capable of supporting scientific workflows, but their capabilities across diverse research activities remain difficult to assess systematically. Existing benchmarks often focus on selected scientific capabilities or task types, limiting a comprehensive characterization of scientific agents' strengths and weaknesses in realistic research workflows. We introduce SCI-AGENT-EXAM, a workflow-oriented benchmark comprising 120 expert-curated tasks across 11 scientific fields and 7 research task types defined by the Scientific Workflow Task Taxonomy (SWTT). Each task requires both executable artifacts (e.g., code and scripts) and textual artifacts (e.g., reports and explanations). To evaluate the heterogeneous task outputs, we develop a hybrid evaluation protocol combining deterministic programmatic verification for executable artifacts with a calibrated, expert-validated LLM-based judge for textual artifacts. Beyond outcome assessment, we analyze progress and solution stability along execution trajectories to characterize how agents reach their final results. Experiments with 10 frontier LLM agents reveal substantial performance gaps: GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest overall score of 80.9, yet only 26.0% of its runs exceed the 0.95 success threshold. Further analyses uncover distinct capability profiles across scientific task types and substantial variation in execution efficiency and stability. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/SKYLENAGE-AI/Sci-Agent-Exam.</p></details> |  |
| **[Async-RBench: Benchmarking Asynchronous Task Execution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32429v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute tasks asynchronously: a main agent continues working while its subagents run, and the task state may change before their results arrive. The main agent must decide which work to update or preserve and which checks to repeat, while avoiding prohibited changes. Final task success alone does not reveal whether these results were handled correctly, and natural asynchronous arrivals are hard to reproduce. We introduce Async-RBench, a benchmark for asynchronous task execution in LLM agents, with 200 executable tasks covering eight scenarios. A scenario controller regulates result delivery using predefined task-state conditions, while an Event Response Contract (ERC) specifies what each response must change, preserve, avoid, and re-verify. The Dynamic Replanning Score (DRS) weights the response process and outcome equally and is reported separately from task success. We evaluate nine agents and find DRS scores of 19.2 to 45.1, with the lowest scores on duplicate reports and subagent failures. Under our execution protocol, all nine agents obtain lower task success with asynchronous than with batched delivery (26.0% vs. 29.7% on average). For three agents, prompting the main agent to rebuild its plan after every result improves DRS by only 2.6 to 3.5 points. Together, these findings highlight the challenges of reliable asynchronous task execution and the need to evaluate how agents handle incoming results alongside end-to-end task success.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 16 figures. Code is available at https://github.com/pope8zdt/Async-RBench</p></details> |
| **[LeafSkill: Efficient Leaf-Level Action Grounding for Recursive LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32412v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recursive large language model (LLM) agents solve long-horizon tasks through decomposition, execution, and environmental feedback. Yet after producing an atomic subtask, they often invoke the same large model again to translate a familiar instruction into an executable action. We observe that high-level planning is task-specific, whereas leaf-level action grounding recurs across tasks and can be checked against environment-admitted actions. We introduce LeafSkill, an external reuse layer that retrieves semantically related grounding experience, binds action templates to current objects and admissible actions, and uses a lightweight 1.7B selector to choose a bound action or defer the unchanged subtask to the original agent. Skills remain outside the model parameters, allowing a frozen selector to use post-training additions. Under lexical and syntactic paraphrases, exact matching retrieves none of the reference actions, while semantic retrieval preserves 96.0% and 97.0% candidate coverage; the frozen selector adopts 97.34% of unseen skill types. On an independent confirmation set of 200 Robotouille tasks, LeafSkill completes 137 tasks versus 121 for ReCAP (68.5% vs. 60.5%, exact McNemar p=.0259). On the 106 tasks completed by both, it reduces Qwen3.5-27B tokens by 14.42% and calls by 14.08%. A separate held-out repeat yields a consistent 9.0-point success gain and 18.98% token reduction. Reusable leaf grounding can thus improve task completion while reducing host-model inference without replacing high-level planning.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Faulty Claims to Incorrect Patches: An Empirical Study of Task-Grounded Reasoning in Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32338v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based coding agents increasingly resolve real-world software engineering tasks, yet still fail on many of them. Prior trajectory studies characterize these failures through task-agnostic patterns of agent behavior, such as repeated actions. However, an agent may follow a sound workflow yet act on a faulty understanding of the existing code, the task requirements, or a candidate fix. Reasoning models, which power most state-of-the-art coding agents, reveal this understanding in their native chain-of-thought (CoT), whose claims about the task can be checked against the facts. Yet it remains unclear how many such checkable claims the CoT contains, how many are faulty, and whether faulty claims actually cause incorrect patches. We present the first empirical study of such claims, which we term task-grounded reasoning, analyzing 400 trajectories of four open-weight reasoning models on SWE-bench Pro. We extract atomic claims from the CoT with an LLM, assess them against evidence gathered by an LLM agent, and manually trace the defects of incorrect patches back to faulty claims, confirming each link with a claim-corrected replay. Task-grounded reasoning covers only 18.5% of the CoT tokens, yet yields 191 atomic claims per trajectory, at least 9.6% of which are faulty. Faulty claims also occur in 96.7% of the resolved trajectories, so their mere presence is a weak signal of failure. Yet two thirds of the 188 incorrect patches trace back to faulty claims. Correcting such a claim makes the agent fix the linked defect in 89.0% of the cases, and correcting all such claims resolves 11.7% of the failed tasks. Our findings show that the CoT carries task-specific signals for diagnosing and repairing agent failures, but exploiting them requires pinpointing the few faulty claims that actually cause patch defects.</p></details> |  |
| **[SkillRep: Characterizing and Understanding the Representation Design Space of Agent Skill Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32336v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieving the right skill from a large library is essential for LLM agents, yet requests and skills are written for different purposes and in different words. Existing retrievers narrow this request-skill gap with one or two representation choices each, so it is unknown whether their gains hold in combination. No prior work measures the gap itself, so it is also unclear which part of it each choice narrows. We define a representation design space by five choices -- encoder fine-tuning, query-side and document-side expansion, background projection and a lexical channel -- and evaluate them together for the first time, as modules of one retriever, SkillRep. With a 0.6B encoder and LLM expansion, SkillRep improves MRR@10 on SRA-Bench over the strongest same-size retriever by 7.02 points with the official skill pool and 8.79 with UniPool, a 111,247-skill pool we build from public registries, and is best on SkillsBench. The value of each choice depends on the others. Fine-tuning, for instance, reverses the ranking of the four training-free modules and turns them from complementary into overlapping. To understand why, we measure the gap with three quantities: background, the embedding component that does not separate skills; unsaid vocabulary, the gold skill's content a request leaves unsaid; and dilution, the information lost when a skill is encoded into a single vector. They show which part each module narrows and by what mechanism. This reveals that modules narrowing the same part compete for it, while those narrowing different parts complement each other.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 3 figures. Code: https://github.com/EverMind-AI/SkillCorpus/tree/main/skillrep</p></details> |
| **[TGR-EvoDAG: A DAG-based Multi-Agent Code Evolution Framework for Generative Recommendation on Tencent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32326v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents have enabled automated code evolution for algorithm discovery. However, current recursive self-improvement (RSI) research for sequential recommendation faces a dilemma: general-purpose frameworks lack domain-specific feedback, while recommender-specific frameworks rely on linear evolution. Crucially, naively integrating them fails when applied to Generative Recommendation (GR). Unlike end-to-end models, GR relies on a module-cascaded pipeline (tokenization, pre-training, and alignment). In this paradigm, existing evolution methods suffer from: i) cost blindness, squandering budgets by ignoring stage-asymmetric retraining overheads; and ii) short-sighted evaluation, discarding promising upstream innovations that temporarily regress metrics before downstream adaptation. To bridge these gaps, we propose TGR-EvoDAG, a multi-agent code evolution framework tailored for GR. TGR-EvoDAG models code evolution as a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) to maintain multiple evolution branches and merge complementary innovations, unifying advanced search with rich GR diagnostic feedback. We further introduce stage-aware cost planning to optimize GPU-hour budgets, and active downstream co-adaptation to navigate temporary regressions and unlock cross-stage gains. On public Amazon datasets, TGR-EvoDAG enables the first autonomous evolution of a complete GR pipeline, improving offline metrics by up to 37.4% and Agent4Rec simulated user satisfaction by 19.7% over the strongest baseline. Furthermore, in industrial deployment on Tencent's large-scale commercial media platform, the evolved GR achieves a 12.16% relative improvement in offline Hit@1 and a statistically significant 0.40% increase in online click-through rate (CTR).</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Gain: Measuring Skill Internalization for Agent Skill Retirement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32322v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents retrieve skills from public hubs that hold up to about 100,000 entries and serve many models. As models improve, a hub must find the skills a given model has internalized, meaning it can do without them, and retire them for that model, yet internalization has not been measured with known reliability. Published rules that compare runs with and without a skill retire on the gain alone. The gain is near zero both for an internalized skill and when neither the model nor the skill delivers the capability, a state we call uncovered. We instead withhold the skill, score the model on a task synthesized from it, and trust a high unaided score only when another model gains from the skill, since nearly half of high scores come from tasks no model benefits from. Under four models, a published gain rule applied with one task per skill removes over half of the uncovered skills. On SkillsBench, 28 of its 31 retirements rest on probes no run passed in full, while retiring on the unaided score costs no detectable reward. Unaided verdicts reproduce across tasks more often than the gain rule's (Cohen's kappa 0.42 against 0.23) but differ across models, so a hub must read them per model. We release 1,251 skills with tasks and item scores under four models.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages. Code: https://github.com/EverMind-AI/SkillCorpus/tree/main/skillcorpus/lifecycle Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/EverMind-AI/skillcorpus-lifecycle</p></details> |
| **[Bridging Exploration and Persona: Information-Directed Dueling for Test-Time Agent Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24385v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Effective test-time personalization requires LLM agents to adapt to query-dependent user preferences from lightweight interaction. We study persona selection with binary comparisons between candidate responses as the online signal. We present ID-TAP, a scalable, IDS-inspired neural dueling-bandit framework for large discrete persona spaces. ID-TAP combines a query-conditioned neural utility model with a lightweight Bradley-Terry explorer, and transfers comparison evidence across related queries through similarity-weighted warm starts. The neural model shares statistical strength across queries while preserving query-specific scores, and efficient BT updates score hypothetical challengers. Across seven user-conditioned generation benchmarks under reference- and model-based preference simulators, ID-TAP achieves the strongest aggregate performance among the evaluated baselines within a fixed ten-round comparison budget. These results support information-directed challenger selection as an effective design for test-time persona adaptation.</p></details> |  |
| **[DMAIC-Bench: From Self-Reflection to Self-Restraint for Agents in Multi-Stage Analytical Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32305v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-stage analytical tasks require LLM agents to use intermediate evidence to decide whether to continue, redirect or stop. Self-reflection, in which an agent critiques its output, is widely studied, yet a critique does not show whether evidence changed the next action. We call that capability self-restraint. Existing agent benchmarks largely measure task completion or output quality and leave it untested. DMAIC-Bench evaluates it with 97 seeded tasks across 15 industrial cases in four sectors, built around Lean Six Sigma's explicit statistical prerequisites. Paired counterfactual variants require opposite actions at the same workflow position, distinguishing warranted restraint from indiscriminate stopping. We assess execution traces with a private scorer and final claims through independent expert review. Across 13 models from six providers, execution-level routing accuracy averages 92% on matched GO decisions and 19% on NO-GO decisions, counting any forbidden downstream computation as a route error. Execution traces show agents reporting correct diagnostics before performing analyses that those results disallow. Separately, blinded expert review of the 11 audited agents estimates that reports act on invalid evidence at 47% of reached NO-GO decisions, with an interval from 31% to 61%. Explicit stopping permission helps some models without consistently restoring correct decisions at both GO and NO-GO forks. | **[Gandalf: Flash Shall Not Die! Moreover, we show that ToM2Vec training generalizes to partner model families not seen at train-time, including a proprietary model (GPT-5.4). In our analysis, we provide evidence that the latent tokens encode a ToM, rather than task-specific information: ToM2Vec's tokens improve the agent's ability to directly answer questions about partner beliefs, desires, and next actions.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/nsivaku/tom2vec</p></details> |
| **[FrontierCS 2: A Benchmark for Computer Science R&D](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29932v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can LLM agents make contributions to frontier computer science R&D? We introduce FrontierCS 2, a benchmark of 48 open-ended tasks. We take a paper and the code its authors released and delete the paper's contribution. The deleted code becomes the human reference that sets the bar. Earlier open-ended benchmarks give each agent a problem statement and grade one program after the run. FrontierCS 2 places the agent inside a research environment. The agent inherits a working repository and may submit a patch at any point. For each patch, a judge returns a development-suite score but keeps the evaluator, the held-out final suite and the reference private, and grades the final submission on that suite against the reference. The tasks span 13 domains, from machine-learning systems and high-performance computing to databases, security and robotics. Beyond these 48 tasks, FrontierCS 2 keeps a private set of more than 100 tasks, to be announced in the future. We evaluate 12 agents. The best one is Claude Opus 5.5, passing 58% of its runs. No run passes on 16 tasks. A higher score is not the same as a pass: most runs that beat the reference on the combined final score still fall short on some metric or workload. On a shared capability scale for agents and tasks, the median task is roughly as difficult as what the strongest agent can handle.</p></details> | <details><summary>Websi...</summary><p>Website: https://frontier-cs.org ; Code: https://github.com/FrontierCS/FrontierCS-2</p></details> |
| **[RelAgent: LLM Agents as Data Scientists for Relational Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07840v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Relational learning is a challenging problem that has motivated a wide range of approaches, including graph-based models (e.g., graph neural networks, graph transformers), tabular methods (e.g., tabular foundation models), and sequence-based approaches (e.g., large language models), each with its own advantages and limitations. We propose RelAgent, an LLM-based autonomous data scientist for relational learning, which operates in two phases. In the search phase, an LLM agent uses database, validation, and evaluation workspace tools to construct SQL feature programs and select a predictive model. In the inference phase, the resulting program is executed without further LLM calls. The final predictor consists of SQL queries and a classical model, enabling fast, deterministic, and intrinsically interpretable predictions: features are human-readable queries, and predictions depend only on the resulting query-defined feature map, enabling scalable deployment using standard database systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Replayable Financial Agents: A Determinism-Faithfulness Assurance Harness for Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.15322v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Replaying a financial agent requires specifying the conditions of a rerun, preserving its execution record, and deciding which parts of its behavior to compare. We introduce the Determinism-Faithfulness Assurance Harness (DFAH), a framework and practical protocol for organizing these choices. The framework distinguishes comparisons of final decisions, tool-name sets, and ordered tool names, while treating support from available evidence as a separate evaluation target. We illustrate the framework using 2,957 recorded executions in synthetic compliance-triage and data-operations tasks. One data-operations configuration repeated its recorded decision across all trials on 84% of cases, while its ordered tool-name sequence repeated on 26%. Conversely, some compliance cases retained the same tool sequence while recorded decisions changed. A case-level example shows how repeated labels accompanied different lookups and escalation calls. These observations demonstrate how the comparison level changes the description of repeated behavior. The resulting protocol connects configuration control, preservation of each attempt, comparison of records, and inspection of changed cases. DFAH provides a practical foundation for designing replay experiments and locating execution differences during the development and evaluation of financial agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 2 figures, 5 tables. Replay framework and descriptive experiments in synthetic financial tasks. Includes an offline reproduction package. Original version accepted at the 2nd ICLR Workshop on Advances in Financial AI (2026)</p></details> |
| **[Understanding Tool-Mediated Interaction Failures in Agentic GUI Software: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31698v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly complete tasks through live graphical user interfaces (GUIs). Because the model cannot directly operate the GUI, these systems often rely on tools to capture screenshots or UI trees, translate model outputs into GUI actions, and report whether the interface changed. We study failures where this tool-mediated interaction makes the agent's view of the task diverge from the live GUI. We construct Malva, a defect library with 335 confirmed real-world failures from 40 open-source applications. These failures fall into 11 defect patterns rooted in state representation, state transition, and synchronization problems. They are widespread: 90% of the studied applications contain more than one defect type. They are also often silent: incorrectness appears in 86.6% of failures, including 119 cases that produce wrong behavior without a fail-stop signal. We operationalize this taxonomy with MalvaGuard, a pre-deployment static contract checker that reaches 57.3% precision and 76.8% recall on an additional 20-application corpus.</p></details> | 13 pages |
| **[PersonalAI 2.0: Enhancing knowledge graph traversal/retrieval with planning mechanism for Personalized LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13481v4)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce PersonalAI 2.0 (PAI-2), a novel framework designed to enhance LLM-based systems through integration of external knowledge graphs (KGs). The proposed approach addresses key limitations of existing Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) methods by incorporating a dynamic, multistage pipeline. The central point of the PAI-2 design is its ability to perform adaptive, iterative information search, guided by extracted entities, matched graph vertices and generated clue-queries. Conducted evaluation on five benchmarks (Natural Questions, TriviaQA, HotpotQA, 2WikiMultihopQA and MuSiQue) demonstrates an improvement in the factual correctness of generated answers compared to baseline methods (LightRAG, RAPTOR, HippoRAG 2 and PAI-1). PAI-2 achieves a 9 percentage points (pp) average gain by LLM-as-a-Judge on 2WikiMultihopQA and MuSiQue benchmarks, and attains comparable accuracy to HippoRAG 2 on the TriviaQA and HotpotQA benchmarks with Qwen2.5 7B and multilingual-e5-large reflecting its effectiveness in reducing hallucination rates and increasing recall. We show that enabled search plan enhancement mechanism gain in average a 18 pp boost compared to disabled one by LLM-as-a-Judge across five benchmarks. In addition, an ablation study reveals that PAI-2 achieves superior result on the MINE-1 benchmark, obtaining an 89\% information-retention score with Qwen2.5 7B and multilingual-e5-large. Collectively, these findings underscore the potential of PAI-2 to serve as a reusable component for personalized AI applications, which require context-aware knowledge-representation and reasoning capabilities. The source code of PAI-2 is available at the following link: https://github.com/Dzigen/PersonalAI.</p></details> |  |
| **[Feedback That Backfires in Games: Do LLM Players Repeat the Move They Just Watched Fail?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30205v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When an LLM agent's move fails, the harness writes the move and the failure message into the transcript and asks again. In tool calling, this record makes small models more likely to repeat the failed action. We test whether the same holds in games (Sokoban, Connect Four, Othello), where a move can fail by being illegal or by being legal but losing, both verified exactly. On fixed probe items built by perturbing a correct move, the record backfires: pooled over 8 instruction-tuned models (135M-1.7B) it makes the failed move more likely (corrective gain G = -1.38 nats per token), through the move's surface form, for illegal moves but not for losing ones, and not for every model. An exploratory analysis shows what sets the sign: after the record, the failed move's log-probability barely depends on how likely it was before (slope 0.18). Moves the model would not have played become more likely, and greedy decoding starts to produce them; moves it was about to play become less likely, but greedy decoding still repeats 38% of them. A description of the failure that omits the move string keeps the model's preference instead. Along the OLMo 2 1B post-training ladder the inversion is present in the base model and largely removed at the DPO stage. RAGEN's default models invert in RAGEN's own Sokoban format before any RL. A "do not repeat" instruction does nothing, a clean retry is the worst harness, and a decoder ban stops exact repeats but not illegal play. All measurements ran in float32 on a free Colab GPU, which first reproduced the original paper's number.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 6 figures. Code, probe items and raw records: https://github.com/Esmail-ibraheem/feedback-that-backfires/tree/games</p></details> |
| **[Effects of Theory of Mind and Prosocial Beliefs on Steering Human-Aligned Behaviors of LLMs in Ultimatum Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24255v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown potential in simulating human behaviors and performing theory-of-mind (ToM) reasoning, crucial for complex social interactions. We investigate ToM reasoning's role in aligning agentic behaviors with human norms in negotiation tasks, using the ultimatum game as our referenced task. We initialized LLM agents with different prosocial beliefs (Greedy, Fair, Selfless) and reasoning methods (chain of thought and ToM reasoning of varying levels), examining their decision-making processes and outcomes across multiple LLMs, including reasoning models like o3-mini and DeepSeek-R1 Distilled Qwen 32B. We perform 2,700 simulations to show that ToM reasoning enhances behavioral alignment with human norms, decision-making consistency, and negotiation outcomes. Consistent with prior findings, reasoning LLMs exhibit limited capability compared to ToM-enhanced LLMs, with different game roles benefiting from different ToM orders. Agents almost always chose strategies consistent with their assigned beliefs, and doing so aligned better with human norms in nearly every role, yet Greedy and Selfless agents still fell short of their norms in outcomes. Human verification further revealed that Llama 3.3 70B produces reasoning most consistent with its actions and beliefs. Our findings advance understanding of ToM's role in human-AI interaction and cooperative decision-making. The code used for our experiments can be found at https://github.com/Stealth-py/UltimatumToM.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to AACL-IJCNLP 2026. v2 corrects the RQ4 analysis (Section 5.4, Fig. 4); other results remain unchanged</p></details> |
| **[DynaWeave: Learning to Adapt through Dynamic Scenario Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29443v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use tools to complete tasks in stateful environments. However, many training environments remain largely static apart from changes caused by the agent's own actions, whereas external events introduced by other users, services, or sudden contingencies in real environments can invalidate ongoing plans. To address this gap, we introduce DynaWeave, an automated synthesis framework that transforms existing static tasks into verifiable dynamic scenarios. The framework generates exogenous events relevant to each task, covering changes in task requirements, environment state, and external information. It uses directed acyclic graphs (DAGs) to represent dependencies between events and an execution engine to activate them during agent interaction. A dynamic verifier revises only the evaluation checks affected by events that actually occur and retains all other checks. Using DynaWeave, we collect 2,095 verified interaction trajectories across 36 environments and use them for supervised fine-tuning of Qwen3.5 models. Experiments on Gaia2, CostBench, and ToolMaze show that DynaWeave achieves consistent improvements in overall performance across all three evaluated model sizes, increasing the task completion rate on CostBench by 67.34 percentage points and even exceeding the performance of Gemini-3.1-Pro-Preview on ToolMaze. These results support dynamic scenario synthesis as a practical approach to training agents to adapt to changing environments. Our code is available at https://github.com/Miaow-Lab/DynaWeave.</p></details> |  |
| **[One Act, Many Masks: Effect-Marginal Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29427v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) have substantially improved the performance of LLM agents on complex, long-horizon interactive tasks. However, despite progress in fine-grained credit assignment, policy optimization still applies these learning signals through response-specific probability ratios. Different textual responses can induce the same executable effect, yet credit differences arising from subsequent outcomes remain tied to their individual textual realizations. We identify this mismatch as credit-update misalignment. Our analysis reveals that credit-text pairing directly shapes policy-update directions, even when the policy context and executable effect are held fixed. Motivated by this finding, we propose Effect-Marginal Policy Optimization (EMPO), which aligns policy optimization with execution-level equivalence through an effect-level surrogate that couples updates across alternative textual realizations. EMPO preserves the original credit signals, integrates with both trajectory-level and step-level credit estimators, and requires no additional critics or rollouts. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Sokoban show that EMPO outperforms strong baselines in both task performance and learning efficiency.</p></details> | 20 pages, 11 figures |
| **[Benchmarking and Error Analysis of Deep Search and Deep Research LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29346v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As Large Language Models (LLMs) evolve into autonomous Deep Search and Deep Research Agents capable of executing long-horizon, multi-step tasks, evaluating their reliability becomes increasingly critical. However, existing evaluation paradigms predominantly focus on final task outcomes, leaving a significant gap in understanding the intermediate execution traces, where agents are highly susceptible to various errors. To address this critical gap, we introduce a comprehensive methodology and analytical framework designed specifically to diagnose errors in intermediate workflows. Building upon this framework, we establish a structured taxonomy that categorizes these vulnerabilities into two primary dimensions: System-Level Errors and Task-Level Errors. Through a systematic examination of step-by-step execution traces and agent-environment interactions, we reveal how these underlying errors affect overall performance. Finally, through empirical experiments and systematic analysis of the identified error types, we provide actionable design guidelines, aiming to enhance the robustness, reasoning capabilities, and reliability of Deep Search and Deep Research agents.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proce...</summary><p>Proceedings of the 1st ICML Workshop on Failure Modes in Agentic AI (FAGEN), Seoul, South Korea, 2026</p></details> |
| **[From Prompts to Posteriors: Can LLM Agents Perform Bayesian Inference in Context?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29331v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied across the scientific discovery pipeline, from synthesizing literature to generating hypotheses to informing experimental design. A unifying principle behind these tasks is the need to continuously update beliefs as evidence accumulates, for which Bayesian inference provides a natural framework. However, existing work assumes that LLMs cannot carry out principled Bayesian belief updating in context, instead relying on simplifications and heuristics. To challenge this assumption, we present a principled evaluation of Bayesian reasoning capabilities in LLMs, analyzing both the model's internal consistency as well as its ability to recover an externally determined posterior. Specifically, we build an evaluation framework spanning qualitative epistemological examples to probe probabilistic coherence, quantitative benchmarks of Bayesian inference capabilities, and analyses of the impact of non-Bayesian updates on downstream task performance. Across different scenarios, we find that LLMs generally violate Bayes' rule when updating their beliefs in context. However, when equipped with modern reasoning abilities and code execution capabilities, their performance drastically improves, enabling even the close approximation of analytically intractable posteriors. Our evaluation shows that current LLMs with access to appropriate tools could already be used to make complex belief updates in scientific discovery, enabling rapid acceleration of the efficiency and scale of AI-powered scientific discovery.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 6 figures in main text</p></details> |
| **[Seeing the Forest for the Trees: Emergent Agreement from Individual Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29328v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM agents interact in the wild and shape each other's decisions, understanding how they converge toward agreement becomes important. We study this in multi-turn conversations where two LLMs discuss subjective matters, can agree or disagree, and may change their stances. Using a linear classifier on individual conversation activations, we identify an internal representation of agreement. We find that it varies substantially across turns and conversations and does not reliably track external agreement at the individual conversation level. We therefore move beyond the representation of agreement in individual conversations, by aggregating the agreement representation across conversations. Externally, this aggregate view reveals a simple structure: when pooled across conversations, agreement can be predicted well with a Markov process, with agreement at each turn being a direct function of agreement at the previous turn. Similarly, when internal agreement is aggregated, it couples with the external Markov agreement through a power-law mapping, exposing Markovian dynamics in internal representations. Additionally, we find that after a change of stance, models express agreement externally before their internal representations reflect the same shift. These results reveal a joint structure between internal and external agreement that emerges only when internal features are aggregated across conversations. Code available at https://github.com/technion-cs-nlp/SeeingTheForestForTheTrees.</p></details> |  |

## Medical Large Language Models
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[PFedQFT: Personalized Federated Quantum-Inspired Fine-Tuning for Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25712v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical data remain fragmented across institutional silos because of privacy, governance, and ownership constraints. Federated learning enables medical institutions to adapt large language models (LLMs) collaboratively without transferring raw records. However, independently parameterized low-rank adapters incur repeated parameter and communication costs across Transformer projections, while uniform aggregation may limit adaptation to heterogeneous institutional data. We propose PFedQFT, a quantum-inspired framework for personalized federated fine-tuning with a compact shared adapter. Specifically, PFedQFT constructs reusable orthonormal adaptation bases from compact skew-symmetric generators using a truncated matrix-exponential approximation followed by QR orthonormalization. Compatible projections reuse these bases, while a globally shared full core preserves the flexibility of each projection. Each client further maintains a lightweight private residual operator, whose directions are learned globally and whose coefficients remain local. Experiments on seven public medical tasks and multiple LLM backbones show substantial reductions in trainable parameters and per-round adapter upload volume, with competitive predictive performance on most tasks.</p></details> | 10 pages, 5 figures |
| **[TriageRA-CCF: Source-Side Clinical Confidence and Coverage Signals for Adaptive Rank Budgeting in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29375v2)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models are commonly adapted with a fixed low-rank budget, even though medical questions differ substantially in confidence, clinical coverage, and cross-domain difficulty. We study adaptive rank budgeting for parameter-efficient medical question answering: for each question, the adapter decides whether to activate a small, medium, or large subset of LoRA rank channels. The central challenge is that a naive adaptive budget router can collapse to unstable choices or spend capacity without improving shifted benchmarks. We propose TriageRA-CCF, a source-side teacher for adaptive rank-budgeted LoRA. It combines three signals computed only from source training data: base-model answer confidence, metadata-cell clinical coverage, and a counterfactual close-miss proxy. These signals supervise a straight-through budget router over active ranks {2,4,8}, together with budget-cost, entropy, and rank-balance regularization. Under a matched CMB-source training protocol, TriageRA-CCF achieves the best average accuracy among LoRA, DoRA, and MoELoRA baselines on both Qwen3-8B and Llama3.1-8B. The gains are modest and non-uniform across benchmarks: +0.21 average points over the strongest external baseline on Qwen3-8B and +0.16 on Llama3.1-8B. Component ablations show that confidence, coverage, and counterfactual signals all provide useful budget supervision, but their combination is not monotonically best on every backbone.</p></details> |  |
| **[MedRouter: Demystifying Knowledge Differences Across Medical LLMs for Routing-Based Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33119v1)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical question answering spans diverse specialties and modalities, and individual medical large language models (LLMs) exhibit distinct strengths across tasks and domains. This heterogeneity suggests that combining specialists may enable broader coverage of medical questions than relying on any single model. However, existing LLM routing methods primarily seek to balance answer quality and inference cost, leaving open how to exploit differences in specialist competence to improve medical reasoning. In this paper, we introduce MedRouter, an agentic system that uses an embedding-based multi-label router to select and query specialist LLMs, then passes their responses to a generator to produce the final answer. We further propose SCALE (Specialist Competence-Aware Learning), a two-stage training framework that first trains the Router with specialist correctness supervision and then optimizes its selections through reinforcement learning. The second stage uses a Performance Gain Reward (PGR) that measures how specialist information affects the generator's answer correctness relative to answering without that information. Experiments on eight text-based and multimodal medical QA benchmarks show that MedRouter outperforms the strongest routing baseline by 8% in average accuracy. Our analysis of specialist outputs further reveals distinct strengths and complementary question-level coverage, motivating learned routing to combine these capabilities for more comprehensive medical reasoning.</p></details> |  |
| **[HiMed: Incentivizing Hindi Reasoning in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24635v2)** | 2026-09-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models hold promise for reducing healthcare disparities, yet Hindi remains severely underrepresented. While medical LLMs excel in high-resource languages, their performance degrades sharply in Hindi, particularly on Indian systems of medicine. We argue that robust cross-lingual medical transfer requires Hindi reasoning. To this end, we introduce HiMed, a Hindi reasoning medical corpus and benchmark suite covering both Western and Indian medicine. We further propose HiMed-8B, a Hindi-form medical reasoning LLM, through the design of decaying scaffolding reward. Extensive experiments demonstrate improvement in Hindi medical reasoning performance and a reduction in the English-Hindi accuracy gap. Ablation studies validate the contribution of each training stage and reward component. All code, data, and weights are available</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[A General Multimodal Probing Framework for Data Fusion and Prediction with Frozen Large Language Models: Evidence from Multimodal EHR Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28798v2)** | 2026-09-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The main objective of this paper is to propose a general framework for prediction based on different sources of multimodal data in the healthcare domain. We evaluated whether frozen medical large language model (LLM) representations can serve as a shared embedding space for multimodal primary diagnosis category prediction. We propose a backbone-flexible probing framework that serializes structured electronic health record (EHR) variables and leakage-pruned discharge-note sections into a shared frozen-LLM representation space and recovers primary diagnosis categories with linear probes, without fine-tuning the LLM. On a MIMIC-IV cohort of 13,645 admissions (seven diagnosis categories), we probed inputs of different modalities (Structured-only, Unstructured-only, and Combined) across transformer layers and six frozen backbones, compared against XGBoost and three text baselines (CAML, LAAT, PLM-ICD) under a matched split and context budget, and tested robustness across datasets on MIMIC-III. The Combined terminal-layer probe reached 88.86% top-1 accuracy on MIMIC-IV, significantly outperforming CAML and PLM-ICD and statistically comparable to LAAT; Combined accuracy ranged from 87.69% to 89.25% across six backbones. On MIMIC-III, in-domain Combined probing reached 74.18% accuracy and significantly exceeded all three baselines, and in a transfer-learning test a 2.8M-parameter adapter on frozen MIMIC-IV representations reached 92.51% accuracy, exceeding adapted LAAT (85.54%). Frozen LLM embeddings unify structured and narrative EHR information: the Combined configuration was strongest in every setting, and its representations transferred across datasets through a compact adapter. Multimodal probing of frozen LLM representations provides a practical, backbone-flexible approach for studying EHR modalities and adapting clinical representations across datasets.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages (14 main text + 16 supplementary), 3 figures, 4 tables; supplementary materials appended after the references</p></details> |
| **[The widening evaluation gap in medical large language model research 2023 to 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11770v1)** | 2026-09-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are superseded every few quarters; clinical evidence takes years. We asked whether medical research is keeping pace with the systems it evaluates. PubMed returned 11,628 records for January 2023 to June 2026 across fourteen clinical domains, growing 45-fold; 2.5% used a randomised, controlled or prospective design. Evaluation lag, from a study's newest named model release to its own publication, widened from 1.33 to 6.08 quarters. Because discontinued models age mechanically, we benchmarked this against a counterfactual holding model composition fixed: migration to newer systems offset only 56% of the drift (95% CI 50-65). Randomised trials evaluated models a median 4.6 quarters older than other designs (P = 3 x 10^-19), yet among studies naming a model still under development no design differed from any other; 62% of randomised trials evaluated a discontinued family. Rigour and currency are in tension, and that tension reflects model selection rather than research timelines.</p></details> |  |
| **[VeriSim: A Configurable Framework for Stress-Testing Medical AI Under Patient Communication Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10441v2)** | 2026-09-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models are typically evaluated on idealized patient cases that do not reflect how real patients communicate. We introduce VeriSim, a patient simulation framework that injects controllable noise along six clinically grounded communication dimensions while substantially preserving each patient's medical record. Truth adherence is supported by a verifier that extracts atomic claims from each candidate utterance and judges them against a UMLS-grounded vector index built with BioLORD embeddings, using the retrieved atoms' structured clinical metadata (e.g., drug class, anatomical site, treats-condition relations) rather than surface-text similarity alone. Across seven open-weight LLMs, realistic noise reduces diagnostic accuracy by 15-25 percentage points and increases conversation length by 34-55%; the 7-8B models degrade 1.4x more than 70B+ models. A board-certified physician and a licensed nurse rate VeriSim's conversations highly on truth, realism, clinical utility, and noise fidelity (inter-annotator agreement >= 0.80 across all dimensions), and an LLM-as-judge closely tracks their ratings, enabling scalable evaluation. We release VeriSim as an open-source framework for rigorous stress-testing of clinical AI: https://github.com/mohitmarvania/VeriSim</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 9 figures, 13 tables. Accepted to Findings of EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Benchmarking Clinical Decision Pathway Adherence in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26592v1)** | 2026-08-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Following clinical decision pathways (CDPs) defined by clinical practice guidelines is essential for safe and reliable medical decision-making. However, existing medical large language model (LLM) benchmarks mainly evaluate final-answer accuracy, providing limited evaluation of models' ability to adhere to guidelines. To address this gap, we introduce MEGA-CDP, a benchmark for evaluating whether medical LLMs can generate guideline-adherent CDPs using provided guidelines as references. MEGA-CDP is constructed from 2,274 English and Chinese clinical practice guidelines through a guideline-to-case pipeline, yielding 42,353 clinical cases with explicit reference CDPs. It supports both single-turn vignette and multi-turn interactive settings, and introduces a CDP-oriented evaluation framework for measuring pathway consistency. Experiments on 16 representative LLMs show that reliable clinical decision support remains challenging for current models, demonstrating the need for CDP-oriented evaluation and the value of MEGA-CDP for advancing guideline adherence in medical LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Didactic knowledge or Clinical Cases? How Data Types Shape Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22161v1)** | 2026-08-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models are commonly trained on mixtures of didactic data (e.g., textbooks) and clinical data (e.g., patient records), yet how these data types differentially shape model capabilities remains unclear. We address this issue with token-matched experiments that vary the didactic-to-clinical ratio and analyze how data composition affects performance, capability profiles, and error patterns across knowledge-intensive and clinic-oriented tasks. We uncover an asymmetric transfer across task types: clinical data improves clinic-oriented tasks while remaining competitive on knowledge-intensive ones, whereas didactic data mainly improves knowledge-intensive tasks. Error analysis suggests a knowing-doing gap, where improvements in knowledge recall do not reliably generalize to clinical reasoning. We further observe that modest amounts of clinical data yield most of the gains on EHR-grounded tasks, while the optimal mixture ratio varies with the knowledge and clinical reasoning demands of downstream tasks. These findings suggest that medical LLM data curation should be application-driven, with higher proportions of clinical data preferred for reasoning-intensive use cases.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This paper is accepted by NLPCC 2026 oral</p></details> |
| **[SDoH-Aware Narrative Anchoring Bias in Medical LLMs for Trustworthy Clinical Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22802v1)** | 2026-08-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models are often judged by how many clinical questions they answer correctly. That view is useful, but it misses a practical risk. A model may know the right answer and still change its response when the same case is written in a different patient voice. This paper evaluates that risk as SDoH aware narrative anchoring bias. We use NarrativeShield SDoH MedQA, a counterfactual medical question answering dataset in which each case appears in persona based narratives while the answer key remains fixed. The dataset is reshaped from wide format into case grouped persona rows. We evaluate three open source instruction tuned LLMs from the Qwen2.5 family: 1.5B, 3B, and 7B. The final experiment uses 300 clinical cases and produces 8,100 model responses across three prompting conditions. We report persona level accuracy, counterfactual consistency, correct consistency, and narrative sensitivity error. Qwen2.5 7B achieves the best accuracy at 56.33 percent and the best correct consistency at 40.33 percent. Paired McNemar exact tests show significant accuracy gains for 7B over 3B in all prompt settings. Even so, narrative sensitivity remains, with the lowest error still at 31.67 percent. Extensive evaluations on four different benchmarks have demonstrated that our developed method consistently and significantly outperforms current TTRL and standalone PRM selection. Our findings establish that transitioning from stochastic heuristics to structured, step-wise rewards is essential for developing reliable and scalable medical AI systems</p></details> |  |
| **[Silent Sabotage During Fine-Tuning: Few-Shot Rationale Poisoning of Compact Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02262v1)** | 2026-02-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is essential for the development of medical large language models (LLMs), yet prior poisoning studies have mainly focused on the detectable backdoor attacks. We propose a novel poisoning attack targeting the reasoning process of medical LLMs during SFT. Unlike backdoor attacks, our method injects poisoned rationales into few-shot training data, leading to stealthy degradation of model performance on targeted medical topics. Results showed that knowledge overwriting was ineffective, while rationale poisoning caused significant decline on the accuracy of the target subject, as long as no correct samples of the same subject appear in the dataset. A minimum number and ratio of poisoned samples was needed to carry out an effective and stealthy attack, which was more efficient and accurate than catastrophic forgetting. We demonstrate though this study the risk of SFT-stage poisoning, hoping to spur more studies of defense in the sensitive medical domain.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeVisE: Behavioral Testing of Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15339v3)** | 2026-02-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied in clinical decision support, yet current evaluations rarely reveal whether their outputs reflect genuine medical reasoning or superficial correlations. We introduce DeVisE (Demographics and Vital signs Evaluation), a behavioral testing framework that probes fine-grained clinical understanding through controlled counterfactuals. Using intensive care unit (ICU) discharge notes from MIMIC-IV, we construct both raw (real-world) and template-based (synthetic) variants with single-variable perturbations in demographic (age, gender, ethnicity) and vital sign attributes. We evaluate eight LLMs, spanning general-purpose and medical variants, under zero-shot setting. Model behavior is analyzed through (1) input-level sensitivity, capturing how counterfactuals alter perplexity, and (2) downstream reasoning, measuring their effect on predicted ICU length-of-stay and mortality. Overall, our results show that standard task metrics obscure clinically relevant differences in model behavior, with models differing substantially in how consistently and proportionally they adjust predictions to counterfactual perturbations.</p></details> | <details><summary>Camer...</summary><p>Camera-ready version published at Findings of the EACL 2026</p></details> |
| **[PeruMedQA: Benchmarking Large Language Models (LLMs) on Peruvian Medical Exams -- Dataset Construction and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11517v2)** | 2026-02-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>BACKGROUND: Medical large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in answering medical examinations. However, the extent to which this high performance is transferable to medical questions in Spanish and from a Latin American country remains unexplored. This knowledge is crucial as LLM-based medical applications gain traction in Latin America. AIMS: To build a dataset of questions medical examinations taken by Peruvian physicians pursuing specialty training; to fine-tune a LLM on this dataset; to evaluate and compare the performance in terms of accuracy between vanilla LLMs and the fine-tuned LLM. METHODS: We curated PeruMedQA, a multiple-choice question-answering (MCQA) dataset containing 8,380 questions spanning 12 specialties (2018-2025). We selected ten medical LLMs, including medgemma-4b-it and medgemma-27b-text-it, and developed zero-shot task specific prompts to answer the questions. We employed parameter-efficient fine tuning (PEFT) and low-rand adaptation (LoRA) to fine-tune medgemma-4b-it utilizing all questions except those from 2025 (test set). RESULTS: Medgemma-27b showed the highest accuracy across all specialities, achieving the highest score of 89.29% in Psychiatry; yet, in two specialties, OctoMed-7B exhibited slight superiority: Neurosurgery with 77.27% and 77.38, respectively; and Radiology with 76.13% and 77.39%, respectively. Across specialties, most LLMs with <10 billion parameters exhibited <50% of correct answers. The fine-tuned version of medgemma-4b-it emerged victorious against all LLMs with <10 billion parameters and rivaled a LLM with 70 billion parameters across various examinations. CONCLUSIONS: For medical AI applications and research that require knowledge bases from Spanish-speaking countries and those exhibiting similar epidemiological profile to Peru's, interested parties should utilize medgemma-27b-text-it.</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://github.com/rodrigo-carrillo/PeruMedQA</p></details> |
| **[MedDialogRubrics: A Comprehensive Benchmark and Evaluation Framework for Multi-turn Medical Consultations in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03023v2)** | 2026-01-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical conversational AI (AI) plays a pivotal role in the development of safer and more effective medical dialogue systems. However, existing benchmarks and evaluation frameworks for assessing the information-gathering and diagnostic reasoning abilities of medical large language models (LLMs) have not been rigorously evaluated. To address these gaps, we present MedDialogRubrics, a novel benchmark comprising 5,200 synthetically constructed patient cases and over 60,000 fine-grained evaluation rubrics generated by LLMs and subsequently refined by clinical experts, specifically designed to assess the multi-turn diagnostic capabilities of LLM. Our framework employs a multi-agent system to synthesize realistic patient records and chief complaints from underlying disease knowledge without accessing real-world electronic health records, thereby mitigating privacy and data-governance concerns. We design a robust Patient Agent that is limited to a set of atomic medical facts and augmented with a dynamic guidance mechanism that continuously detects and corrects hallucinations throughout the dialogue, ensuring internal coherence and clinical plausibility of the simulated cases. Furthermore, we propose a structured LLM-based and expert-annotated rubric-generation pipeline that retrieves Evidence-Based Medicine (EBM) guidelines and utilizes the reject sampling to derive a prioritized set of rubric items ("must-ask" items) for each case. We perform a comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art models and demonstrate that, across multiple assessment dimensions, current models face substantial challenges. Our results indicate that improving medical dialogue will require advances in dialogue management architectures, not just incremental tuning of the base-model.</p></details> |  |
| **[Measuring Stability Beyond Accuracy in Small Open-Source Medical Large Language Models for Pediatric Endocrinology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11567v1)** | 2025-12-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Small open-source medical large language models (LLMs) offer promising opportunities for low-resource deployment and broader accessibility. However, their evaluation is often limited to accuracy on medical multiple choice question (MCQ) benchmarks, and lacks evaluation of consistency, robustness, or reasoning behavior. We use MCQ coupled to human evaluation and clinical review to assess six small open-source medical LLMs (HuatuoGPT-o1 (Chen 2024), Diabetica-7B, Diabetica-o1 (Wei 2024), Meditron3-8B (Sallinen2025), MedFound-7B (Liu 2025), and ClinicaGPT-base-zh (Wang 2023)) in pediatric endocrinology.
